=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are N slimes lined up in a row from left to right.
For i = 1, 2, \ldots, N, the i-th slime from the left has size A_i.
For each K = 1, 2, \ldots, N, solve the following problem.

Takahashi is the K-th slime from the left in the initial state.
Find the maximum size that he can have after performing the following action any number of times, possibly zero:

- Choose a slime adjacent to him that is strictly smaller than him, and absorb it.
As a result, the absorbed slime disappears, and Takahashi's size increases by the size of the absorbed slime.

When a slime disappears due to absorption, the gap is immediately closed, and the slimes that were adjacent to the disappearing slime (if they exist) become adjacent (see the explanation in Sample Input 1).

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the answers B_K for each K = 1, 2, \ldots, N, separated by spaces, in the following format:
B_1 B_2 \ldots B_N

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 5 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 10^9
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

6
4 13 2 3 2 6

Sample Output 1

4 30 2 13 2 13

As an example, consider the problem for K = 4.
We will refer to the sizes of the remaining slimes, listed from left to right, with Takahashi's size enclosed in square brackets [ ], as the state of the row.
That is, the initial state is (4, 13, 2, [3], 2, 6). Consider the following sequence of actions by Takahashi.

- He absorbs the slime to his right. As a result, the absorbed slime disappears, and his size becomes 3 + 2 = 5. The state becomes (4, 13, 2, [5], 6).
- He absorbs the slime to his left. As a result, the absorbed slime disappears, and his size becomes 5 + 2 = 7. The state becomes (4, 13, [7], 6).
- He absorbs the slime to his right. As a result, the absorbed slime disappears, and his size becomes 7 + 6 = 13. The state becomes (4, 13, [13]).

There are no slimes adjacent to him that are strictly smaller than him, so he cannot perform any more actions. His final size is 13, which is the maximum possible.

Sample Input 2

12
22 25 61 10 21 37 2 14 5 8 6 24

Sample Output 2

22 47 235 10 31 235 2 235 5 235 6 235

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's formalize the multiset deletion problem (ignoring boundary for a moment): Given multiset of numbers with total S, can we delete all elements except one distinguished K (not deleted) such that each deleted element x is < remaining total - x (i.e., x < half of remaining total including x)? Equivalently, at each deletion, the deleted element's value is less than the sum of all other remaining elements (including K and others). This is possible iff? Let's analyze.

Let T be total sum of all elements including K. We need delete all non-K. Suppose we have a deletion order. Consider the last deleted element x. Just before deleting x, remaining elements are x and K (since all others deleted). Condition: x < K. Thus the last deleted element must be smaller than K. This is necessary. More generally, consider the moment just before deleting an element x; the remaining total R = x + (sum of other remaining). Need x < sum other remaining. This means x is not a majority of the remaining multiset. If we sort deletion order, each deleted element must be less than the sum of all elements deleted after it plus K. Let d_1, d_2, ..., d_m be deletion order (reverse of absorption). Condition for d_j: d_j < K + sum_{t>j} d_t. Equivalently, if we process from last to first (forward absorption), d_m (last deleted = first absorbed? Wait forward absorption order is reverse of deletion. Let's define forward order e_1,...,e_m (absorption order). Reverse deletion order is d_1=e_m, d_2=e_{m-1},..., d_m=e_1. Condition for deletion d_j: d_j < K + sum_{t>j} d_t. In terms of forward, for e_i (absorbed at step i), before absorption current sum = K + sum_{t<i} e_t. Need e_i < K + sum_{t<i} e_t. Same original. OK.

For multiset deletion, condition for existence of order is equivalent to: There exists an ordering of non-K elements such that for each prefix in forward? Actually forward condition: each new element < current sum. This is exactly the same as original but without boundary adjacency. For a set, if you can choose any element, what is condition? This is known: Starting with capital K, you can add items if item value < current capital, capital += value. Can you add all items? This is possible iff after sorting items ascending, each item < current sum. Since if any item is addable, adding smaller items first maximizes growth. Greedy: sort items ascending, add if value < current sum. If at some point smallest remaining >= current sum, impossible. This is classic. The condition is that for every item, its value < sum of K plus all smaller items? More precisely, if sorted ascending b1<=...<=bm, need b_i < K + sum_{j<i} b_j for all i. This is necessary and sufficient for unrestricted order. Because adding smaller first is always optimal. Great.

Add 5 ->11, add5 ->16, next100: 100<16 false, impossible. So unrestricted condition fails. Final interval [3,3] only. Sample K=4 interval [3,6] non-K sorted [2,2,6], K=3. Add2 ->5, add2 ->7, add6 ->13, success. K=2 all non-K sorted [2,2,3,4,6], K=13, success. K=6 final [3,6] non-K sorted [2,2,3], K=6, add2,2,3 success. Good.

But is boundary constraint ever stricter than unrestricted sorted condition? Consider interval [100, 1, 1, 100] with K=1? Start K=100 at left, interval all non-K sorted [1,1,100], K=100. Unrestricted: add1,1,100 success (100+1+1=102>100). Boundary: start at left 100, adjacent right1 <100 absorb ->101, next right1 ->102, next right100? 100<102 yes, success. OK.

Consider K in middle with large elements on both sides, but unrestricted condition might allow because you can add all small elements first, but if small elements are behind large barriers, boundary prevents. Example [100, 1, 6, 1, 100], K=6 at center. Non-K sorted [1,1,100,100], K=6. Unrestricted: add1 ->7, add1 ->8, next100 <8 false, impossible. So not allowed.

Need example where unrestricted condition holds but boundary fails. Need small elements accessible? Suppose K=10, array [100, 1, 10, 1, 100]? Non-K sorted [1,1,100,100], K=10: add1,1 ->12, next100<12 false, fails. Need enough small sum to exceed large barriers. If there are large barriers on both sides, to absorb one barrier need current sum >100; small elements accessible before crossing barriers are only those on same side? Actually if K has large barriers immediately adjacent, no small accessible. But if there are small elements outside barriers, can't reach them. Unrestricted would include them, but boundary can't. Example K=10, array [1, 100, 10, 100, 1]. Non-K sorted [1,1,100,100], K=10: add1,1 ->12, next100<12 false, fails. Let's verify original: K=10, neighbors 100, 100<10 false, cannot move. So final 10. Thus final interval cannot include outside small elements even though sorted condition holds. So need geometric boundary.

Therefore need account for adjacency: expansion can only cross elements in order; barriers block access to outside.

This suggests a tree structure: To reach an element, you must cross all elements between it and K. The order along each side is fixed. So for each side, the sequence of values from K outward must be addable in that order, possibly interleaved with other side.

This is like two queues. We need compute closure for all K.

Maybe there is a divide-and-conquer approach: For each K, final interval maybe can be found by considering "nearest blocking element" on each side. Let's define for current interval [L,R] with sum S, a boundary element is blocked if A[L-1] >= S or A[R+1] >= S. If both blocked, stop. If one side blocked, must expand other side until S exceeds blocked value, then can cross. Thus final interval is determined by alternating expansions.

Could use a priority queue of boundary values? At each step, if any boundary value < S, absorb it. Since all available boundaries remain available, we can absorb all boundaries with value < S. But after absorbing one, S increases, so more boundaries become available. This is like we have two pointers L,R and a set of boundary values. Since only two boundaries, we can simulate with a while loop: while min(boundary values) < S? Actually if either boundary < S, absorb it. If both < S, which to absorb? Since both available and absorbing one increases S, the other remains available. To maximize speed, absorb both? We can absorb one, S increases, then maybe the other; order doesn't matter for final closure. For simulation, we could absorb the smaller boundary first? Since if a boundary is < S, after absorbing it, S increases. Absorbing smaller first increases S less than absorbing larger first; but both available. Does order affect ability to absorb a newly exposed boundary? Suppose left boundary x<S, right boundary y<S. If absorb x, S' = S+x. New left boundary z may be <S' or not. Right y remains <S'. If absorb y first, S''=S+y, new right boundary w may be <S''. Could there be a z that is <S' but not <S''? Since S''>S' if y>x, so if z<S' then z<S''. If y<x, S' > S'', so absorbing larger first might expose z that is <S' but not <S''; but if we absorb smaller first, we might miss z? Wait if we absorb smaller first, S'=S+x (if x smaller? Actually choose boundary value). Let's set left x=10, right y=100, S=50. Both available. If absorb left (10) first -> S=60, new left z=55 (<60) available, right y=100 not. If absorb right (100) first -> S=150, new right w maybe 140 available, left x=10 available. Both orders may expose different new boundaries. But since all available remain, after absorbing both x and y, S=160, both new boundaries z,w available if <160. So order doesn't matter for final closure if we eventually absorb all available. But to decide whether z is available, after absorbing both x,y, S=160, so z<160. If we only absorbed x, z<60 maybe; but y still available, absorb y ->160, z remains available. Thus any boundary that becomes available after some sequence remains available as S increases. So we can simply at each step absorb any available boundary; final closure unique. For efficient simulation, we can absorb all boundaries with value < current S, but after absorbing one, S increases, so maybe the same boundary's next neighbor becomes available. This is like expanding through a run on each side until hitting a value >= S. Since S increases when absorbing from either side, the stopping value threshold increases.

Maybe we can precompute for each interval [L,R] the maximum S? Hmm.

Let's consider using "monotonic stack of prefix sums" to find when a side can be fully absorbed.

For left expansion: Given current right boundary R and current sum S = P[R]-P[L-1]. To absorb left elements down to L' (new L), condition for each j from L-1 down to L': P[j+1] < P[R] (as derived). This depends only on R, not on L or S! Wait P[R] is the prefix sum at current right boundary. If we have not expanded right, R is fixed. Let left neighbor index j = L-1. Current interval [L,R], sum S = P[R] - P[L-1] (since P[L-1] sum up to L-1). Need A[j] < S.

=> 2P[L-1] - P[L-2] < P[R].

For left expansion from current interval [l,r], before absorbing j = l-1, current interval [j+1, r] = [l,r], sum = P[r] - P[j] (since l = j+1, P[l-1]=P[j]). Need A[j] < P[r] - P[j].

=> 2P[j] - P[j-1] < P[r].

After absorbing j=3, interval [3,4], sum = P4-P2=5. Next left j=2 (A=13), r=4, P[r]=22, P[j]=P2=17, P[j-1]=P1=4. Condition 2*17-4=30 <22 false, so cannot absorb 13. Good. If we had used P[j+1]=P4=22 < P[r]=22 false (strict false), also false. But for j=3, P[j+1]=P4=22 <22 false, would say cannot absorb first left, wrong. So correct condition is 2P[j] - P[j-1] < P[r].

For right expansion, by symmetry, condition for absorbing j = r+1 from current interval [l,r]: current sum = P[j-1] - P[l-1]. Need A[j] < P[j-1] - P[l-1].
A[j] = P[j] - P[j-1].
Condition: P[j] - P[j-1] < P[j-1] - P[l-1]
=> P[j] + P[l-1] < 2P[j-1].
This is symmetric with reversed prefix.

So left expansion condition depends on r and j: 2P[j] - P[j-1] < P[r]. Define Lval[j] = 2P[j] - P[j-1] = P[j] + A[j]? Since P[j]-P[j-1]=A[j], so 2P[j]-P[j-1] = P[j] + A[j]. Yes! Condition to absorb left neighbor j is P[j] + A[j] < P[r]. Wait P[j]+A[j] = P[j+1]? No, P[j+1]=P[j]+A[j+1]. But P[j]+A[j] is not a standard prefix (it's sum up to j plus A[j]). Let's denote C[j] = P[j] + A[j]. Then condition C[j] < P[r]. For j=3 in sample: P3=19, A3=2, C3=21 < P4=22 true. For j=2: P2=17, A2=13, C2=30 < P4=22 false. Nice.

For right expansion, condition for absorbing j = r+1: P[j] + P[l-1] < 2P[j-1]. Let D[j]? Since P[j] = P[j-1]+A[j], condition P[j-1]+A[j]+P[l-1] < 2P[j-1] => A[j] + P[l-1] < P[j-1]. Or P[j] + P[l-1] < 2P[j-1]. Define maybe using suffix.

So left expansion is easier to characterize with C[j] and P[r]. If current right boundary r is fixed, the set of left indices j that can be absorbed consecutively are those with C[j] < P[r]. Since as we move left, j decreases. Need all j from L-1 down to new L satisfy C[j] < P[r]. C[j] = P[j]+A[j] = sum up to j plus A[j] = P[j-1]+2A[j]. Is C[j] monotonic? P[j] increasing, A[j] arbitrary, so C[j] not necessarily monotonic. Example A=[100,1], P1=100,C1=200; P2=101,C2=102, decreases. So not monotonic. Thus absorbing left run until first C[j] >= P[r]? Wait if C[j] < P[r] for each j. Since C not monotonic, a later j (further left) might have C < P[r] even if an earlier j had C >= P[r]? But to reach further left, you must absorb earlier j first. If earlier j is not absorbable, you cannot skip it. So the left expansion stops at the first j (from current L-1 leftwards) where C[j] >= P[r]. Because if the immediate left neighbor j has C[j] >= P[r], cannot absorb it; even if further left has C < P[r], inaccessible. If immediate neighbor absorbable, absorb it; then next immediate left j-1 condition with same P[r] (since r unchanged) is C[j-1] < P[r]. Thus the entire absorbable left run is the contiguous segment ending at L-1 and extending left while C[j] < P[r]. Since if a later element has C >= P[r], stop; elements beyond cannot be reached. So for fixed r, the left boundary can be moved to the position after the nearest index to the left of current L with C >= P[r]? More precisely, define for each r, the "blocking" left index b = max { j < L : C[j] >= P[r] }? Starting from current L, we can absorb left while C[L-1] < P[r], then L--, repeat. Stop when C[L-1] >= P[r]. So the new L is one plus the nearest index to the left of original L with C >= P[r]? If there is a blocking index j0 < L with C[j0] >= P[r], and no blocking between j0+1 and L-1, then we can absorb down to j0+1. So L_new = j0 + 1. If no blocking left, L_new=1. This is like using a monotonic stack to find previous index with C >= threshold.

Similarly, right expansion for fixed l: condition for absorbing right neighbor j is P[j] + P[l-1] < 2P[j-1]. Define maybe E[j] = 2P[j-1] - P[j] = P[j-1] - A[j]. Condition P[l-1] < E[j]. Because P[j] + P[l-1] < 2P[j-1] => P[l-1] < 2P[j-1] - P[j] = P[j-1] - A[j]. Let E[j] = P[j-1] - A[j]. Then absorb right j iff P[l-1] < E[j]. For fixed l, right expansion stops at first j where E[j] <= P[l-1]. Since E[j] can be negative? Ai positive, P[j-1] could be less than A[j], so E negative. Condition P[l-1] < E[j]. P[l-1] positive. So if E <= P[l-1], cannot absorb. We can find next index to right with E <= threshold.

This is promising! We can model expansion as alternating jumps:
- State (L,R). Current sum S = P[R]-P[L-1].
- If L>1 and C[L-1] < P[R], then we can expand left. The maximal left expansion for current R: find nearest index j < L with C[j] >= P[R]; set L = j+1 (or 1 if none). Because all indices from L-1 down to j+1 have C < P[R].
- If R<N and E[R+1] > P[L-1], then expand right. Find nearest index j > R with E[j] <= P[L-1]; set R = j-1 (or N if none). Because all indices from R+1 up to j-1 have E > P[L-1].

After expanding one side, P[R] or P[L-1] changes, which may allow expansion on the other side. Repeat until no expansion possible.

This looks like a two-dimensional monotone process. Need compute final (L,R) for all K efficiently.

Let's verify formulas with sample.

Sample A: [4,13,2,3,2,6]
P: [0,4,17,19,22,24,30]
C[j]=P[j]+A[j]:
j1:4+4=8
j2:17+13=30
j3:19+2=21
j4:22+3=25
j5:24+2=26
j6:30+6=36
E[j]=P[j-1]-A[j]:
j1:0-4=-4
j2:4-13=-9
j3:17-2=15
j4:19-3=16
j5:22-2=20
j6:24-6=18

Start K=4: L=R=4, P[R]=P4=22, P[L-1]=P3=19.
Left neighbor j=3: C3=21 <22 => expand left. Find nearest j<4 with C>=22. C3=21<22, C2=30>=22, so j=2, L=3. (Absorb index3). Now L=3,R=4. P[R]=22, P[L-1]=P2=17.
Right neighbor j=5: E5=20 > P2=17 => expand right. Find nearest j>4 with E<=17. E5=20>17, E6=18>17, none, R=6. (Absorb 5,6). Now L=3,R=6. P[R]=P6=30, P[L-1]=P2=17.
Left neighbor j=2: C2=30 < P6=30? strict false, stop. Right none. Final [3,6] sum13. Good.

Start K=2: L=R=2, P[R]=17, P[L-1]=P1=4.
Left j=1: C1=8 <17 => expand left. Find nearest j<2 with C>=17: none? C1=8<17, so L=1.
Now L=1,R=2, P[R]=17, P[L-1]=0.
Right j=3: E3=15 >0 => expand right. Find nearest j>2 with E<=0: none? E3=15,E4=16,E5=20,E6=18 all >0, R=6. Final all. Good.

Start K=1: L=R=1, P[R]=4, P[L-1]=0.
Right j=2: E2=-9 >0? false, stop. Final [1,1].

Start K=6: L=R=6, P[R]=30, P[L-1]=P5=24.
Left j=5: C5=26 <30 => expand left. Find nearest j<6 with C>=30: j=2 (C2=30) (j3=21,j4=25,j5=26), so L=3. Now L=3,R=6. P[R]=30, P[L-1]=P2=17.
Left j=2: C2=30 <30 false. Final [3,6]. Good.

So the jump formulation works.

Now we need compute final interval for every K efficiently. This is like a reachability problem in a directed graph on states (L,R) with transitions:
- Left jump: L = prev_ge_C(L-1, P[R]) + 1, where prev_ge_C(x, threshold) = largest index <= x with C[index] >= threshold. If none, 0.
- Right jump: R = next_le_E(R+1, P[L-1]) - 1, where next_le_E(x, threshold) = smallest index >= x with E[index] <= threshold. If none, N+1.

State space O(N^2) impossible. Need exploit structure.

Observe transitions only depend on P[R] and P[L-1]. Let's define l = L-1 (left outside index), r = R. Then interval is (l, r] with l < K <= r. Sum = P[r] - P[l]. State (l,r). Start (K-1, K). Left expansion: if C[l] < P[r], then l becomes prev_ge_C(l, P[r]) (the largest index <= l with C >= P[r]); because L = l+1, new l = L-1 = prev_ge_C(l, P[r]). If no such, l=0. Right expansion: if E[r+1] > P[l], then r becomes next_le_E(r+1, P[l]) - 1? Let's define r is current right boundary. Next right neighbor index r+1. If E[r+1] > P[l], expand right until before first index j with E[j] <= P[l]. New r = next_le_E(r+1, P[l]) - 1. If none, r=N. So state (l,r) with l < K <= r. Transitions:
- l := g(l, P[r]) where g(l, t) = max { i <= l | C[i] >= t } (or 0).
- r := h(r+1, P[l]) - 1 where h(x, t) = min { i >= x | E[i] <= t } (or N+1).

We start at (K-1, K). Need final l,r.

This resembles alternating fixed point. Since l only decreases (or stays), r only increases (or stays). The process terminates when C[l] >= P[r] (if l>0) and E[r+1] <= P[l] (if r<N). Actually stop conditions:
- Left blocked: l==0 or C[l] >= P[r].
- Right blocked: r==N or E[r+1] <= P[l].

Final state is a pair (l,r) with l < K <= r satisfying these and reachable.

Can we compute final state for all K using some data structure and memoization? States are intervals; K lies inside. Maybe final intervals have a laminar/nested structure? Let's examine sample final intervals:
K=1: [1,1] -> l=0,r=1
K=2: [1,6] -> l=0,r=6
K=3: [3,3] -> l=2,r=3
K=4: [3,6] -> l=2,r=6
K=5: [5,5] -> l=4,r=5

Let's attempt to derive an O(N log N) algorithm using the jump formulation and laminar property.

State (l,r) with l < K <= r. l decreases, r increases. The final l,r satisfy:
C[l] >= P[r] (if l>0) and E[r+1] <= P[l] (if r<N). Actually stop condition left: if l>0, C[l] >= P[r]. Right: if r<N, E[r+1] <= P[l].

Let's denote l = L-1, r = R. Start (K-1, K). The process alternates:
- While C[l] < P[r], set l = prev_ge_C(l, P[r]).
- While E[r+1] > P[l], set r = next_le_E(r+1, P[l]) - 1.

This is reminiscent of computing the greatest fixed point of two monotone functions. Because l only decreases, r only increases. We can maybe process K in increasing order with a stack.

Observe final l is determined by final r: l = prev_ge_C(l? no, after left expansions, l becomes the largest index <= original l with C >= P[r]? Actually if multiple left expansions occur, l eventually becomes prev_ge_C(initial l, P[final r])? Let's check. Suppose starting l0=K-1. Left expansion uses current r. If r increases later, threshold P[r] increases, so more left elements may become absorbable. The final l after all left expansions should be the largest index <= K-1 with C[index] >= P[final r]? Wait left expansion stops when C[l] >= P[r]. If there is an index j < l with C[j] >= P[r], but l is already after the nearest such index? Let's define final l. Since l only decreases to positions where C[l] >= P[r] at the time of stop. If r later increases, P[r] increases, so it's possible that C[l] < new P[r], then l decreases further to previous index with C >= new P[r]. Thus final l is the largest index <= K-1 such that C[l] >= P[final r]? Actually if final r is fixed, and we want l maximal (closest to K) with C[l] >= P[final r] and l < K? Because to stop left, the immediate left neighbor of final interval is l, and it must be blocked: C[l] >= P[final r]. If there is any index between l+1 and K-1 with C >= P[final r], then l wouldn't be maximal? Wait l is the index just left of final L. But condition for absorbing an inside left index j at final r is C[j] < P[final r] (if r had reached final before absorbing j). However, some inside indices may have been absorbed when r was smaller; but if C[j] >= P[final r], then since P[final r] is larger, C[j] >= larger, so it would also have been blocked at final r, but it was absorbed earlier when P[r] was smaller? Wait condition C[j] < P[r] at the time of absorption. If r later increases, P[r] increases, so if C[j] < old P[r], then C[j] < new P[r] as well. Thus any absorbed left index j has C[j] < P[r_at_time] <= P[final r]? Actually P[r_at_time] <= P[final r] because r only increases, so C[j] < P[r_at_time] <= P[final r]. Therefore C[j] < P[final r]. So all indices inside final interval to the left of K satisfy C < P[final r]. The first index outside l must have C[l] >= P[final r] (if l>0). Therefore final l is indeed the largest index < K with C[index] >= P[final r]? Wait if there is an index j between l+1 and K-1 with C[j] >= P[final r], it couldn't be inside. So none. Thus l = max { j < K | C[j] >= P[final r] } (or 0). This is independent of path! Similarly, final r is the smallest index > K with E[r] <= P[final l]? Let's check. For right side, all absorbed right indices j have E[j] > P[l_at_time]? Condition to absorb right j: E[j] > P[l] (where l is left boundary at time). l only decreases, so P[l] decreases (since l decreases to smaller index, prefix sum decreases). Wait P[l] is sum up to l; as l decreases, P[l] decreases. Condition E[j] > P[l_at_time]. If l later decreases, P[l] decreases, so condition becomes easier. Thus absorbed right indices satisfy E[j] > P[l_at_time] >= P[final l]? Since l_at_time >= final l, P[l_at_time] >= P[final l]. So E[j] > P[l_at_time] >= P[final l], hence E[j] > P[final l]. The first outside right index r+1 must be blocked: E[r+1] <= P[final l]. Thus final r = min { j > K | E[j] <= P[final l] } - 1? More precisely, r is the largest index such that all K+1..r have E > P[final l], and r+1 has E <= P[final l] (or r=N). So r = min { j > K | E[j] <= P[final l] } - 1, with min over j>K, if none then N.

This is huge! It means the final interval [L,R] for K is determined by a pair (l,r) satisfying:
l = prev_C_ge_before_K(P[r])  (largest j < K with C[j] >= P[r], or 0)
r = next_E_le_after_K(P[l]) - 1  (smallest j > K with E[j] <= P[l], minus 1, or N)
and K is between l+1 and r. Moreover, these equations are necessary for any final state, independent of order. Let's verify with sample K=4 final l=2,r=6. P[r]=P6=30. Largest j<4 with C[j]>=30: C2=30, j=2, yes. P[l]=P2=17. Smallest j>4 with E[j]<=17: E5=20>17,E6=18>17, none, r=6, yes.

Excellent! So the final state (l,r) is the unique solution to:
l = f_K(r) := max { j < K | C[j] >= P[r] } (0 if none)
r = g_K(l) := min { j > K | E[j] <= P[l] } - 1 (N if none)
with l < K <= r. And since final interval is stable, these hold.

This is a fixed-point equation. We need compute for each K the pair (l,r). This is reminiscent of "two-dimensional monotone" and can be solved by divide and conquer? Let's define arrays:
C[i] = P[i] + A[i] for i=1..N.
E[i] = P[i-1] - A[i] for i=1..N. Note E can be negative.

For a given K and a threshold T = P[r], l = prev_ge_C(K-1, T): largest index < K with C >= T.
For a given K and threshold U = P[l], r = next_le_E(K+1, U) - 1: smallest index > K with E <= U, minus 1.

Because P is strictly increasing, thresholds are prefix sums.

We can think of each index i having C[i] and E[i]. The final l is determined by final r: l = prev_ge_C(K-1, P[r]). Since r >= K, P[r] >= P[K]. As r increases, threshold increases, so l moves left (non-increasing). The final r is determined by final l: r = next_le_E(K+1, P[l]) - 1. As l decreases, P[l] decreases, so threshold decreases, making next E<= threshold possibly further right? Wait if threshold decreases, fewer E <= threshold, so next_le may move right (r increases). Thus l smaller -> r larger. Conversely r larger -> threshold P[r] larger -> l smaller. There may be multiple fixed points? But process gives maximal? Let's see.

Starting l0=K-1, r0=K. Compute l1 = prev_ge_C(K-1, P[r0]) (left expansion with initial r). This l1 <= l0. Then r1 = next_le_E(K+1, P[l1]) -1 >= r0. Then l2 = prev_ge_C(K-1, P[r1]) <= l1. Then r2 >= r1. This alternates and converges to the least l and greatest r? Since l decreases, r increases. The final is the greatest fixed point? Actually l decreases, r increases until stop. The equations above characterize stop. The iterative process:
l_{t+1} = f_K(r_t)
r_{t+1} = g_K(l_{t+1})? Or after l update, update r. Starting r0=K, l1=f(r0), r1=g(l1), l2=f(r1), ...
Since f is non-increasing in r (larger r -> smaller l), g is non-decreasing in l? g(l) = next_le_E(K+1, P[l]) -1. As l decreases, P[l] decreases, next_le_E with lower threshold is >= (further right), so r increases. Thus g is non-increasing in l? If l decreases, r increases, so g is anti-monotone. Composition f(g(l))? This is like monotone.

Can we compute final pair by binary search? For each K, maybe we can find final r as the smallest r >= K such that something? Let's derive a direct condition.

Final l = prev_ge_C(K-1, P[r]). This means:
- C[l] >= P[r] (if l>0)
- For all j with l < j < K, C[j] < P[r].
Final r = next_le_E(K+1, P[l]) - 1 means:
- E[r+1] <= P[l] (if r<N)
- For all j with K < j <= r, E[j] > P[l].

Thus final pair (l,r) satisfies:
(1) l < K <= r.
(2) If l>0: C[l] >= P[r]; if l=0 no condition.
(3) For all j in (l, K): C[j] < P[r].
(4) If r<N: E[r+1] <= P[l]; if r=N no condition.
(5) For all j in (K, r]: E[j] > P[l].

These are exactly the stability conditions. Additionally, l is the largest index <K with C>=P[r], so (2)(3). r is the smallest index >K with E<=P[l], so (4)(5).

This looks like we can find final interval by scanning outward from K until conditions hold. Maybe use data structures to jump.

- prev_ge_C(pos, threshold): among indices <= pos, find largest index with C >= threshold.
- next_le_E(pos, threshold): among indices >= pos, find smallest index with E <= threshold.

These are classic range successor/predecessor queries with static arrays C,E. Can answer O(log N) with segment tree storing max C and min E. Then for each K, we could simulate alternating jumps. Let's explore laminar tree construction.

Final intervals are laminar. Each interval [L,R] has a "sum" S = P[R]-P[L-1]. Stability: if L>1, A[L-1] >= S; if R<N, A[R+1] >= S. Also, for any K in [L,R], the final interval is the minimal? Consider the final interval for K. It is the closure under expansion. This is like if we start with K and expand to the maximal interval [L,R] such that all elements inside can be absorbed. There is a known algorithm using "union find with priority queue" for "eating slimes" maybe:

Let's look at the equations. For a fixed K, final l is determined by final r. Maybe we can find final r by considering the function F_K(r) = g_K(f_K(r))? Starting r0=K, r_{t+1}=g(f(r_t)). Since r increases. We need fixed point r = g(f(r)). Because f non-increasing, g non-increasing? Let's check monotonicity:
- f(r) = prev_ge_C(K-1, P[r]). As r increases, P[r] increases, so the set {j<=K-1 | C[j]>=P[r]} shrinks, so f(r) decreases (or stays). Thus f is non-increasing.

The maximum element's behavior suggests a recursive decomposition by maximum: The global maximum (or one of them) can absorb all if it has access to a smaller neighbor; if not, it's a barrier. This is like building a Cartesian tree where each node is a maximum that separates intervals.

Let's consider the Cartesian tree of A (max-heap). For any interval, the maximum element acts as a barrier between left and right. To cross a maximum, Takahashi must grow larger than it. If starting on one side, the maximum may be absorbed only after accumulating enough from that side (and possibly the other side? But to absorb the maximum, it must be adjacent; if starting left of max, you can absorb left side, then max is adjacent. You cannot access right side before absorbing max. So the sum available before absorbing max is the sum of the left side interval you've absorbed. Thus you can cross the max iff the sum of the absorbed left side > A_max. If not, max blocks. Similarly from right. This is exactly the Cartesian tree: each maximum separates left and right; to cross it, the source side's accumulated sum must exceed the max's value. If it does, the max is absorbed, and then the combined interval may cross further.

This is promising! Let's formalize using Cartesian tree of A (max). For an interval, the maximum element m splits into left subinterval [L,m-1] and right [m+1,R]. Starting from a position K:
- If K is in left subinterval, to absorb the maximum m, Takahashi must first absorb some interval within left subinterval, and then when m is adjacent, need current sum > A[m]. If it can absorb m, then after absorbing m, it has access to right subinterval as well.
- If K is in right, symmetric.
- If K = m, it starts as the maximum; it can absorb adjacent smaller elements from both sides. Since it's the max, initially it can absorb any neighbor (unless equal max). It may absorb all? If equal maxima, need handle.

This recursive view might yield an algorithm using divide and conquer on the maximum. For each K, the final interval is contained within some "basin" bounded by maxima that are too large to cross.

Let's define for any interval [L,R] with maximum at m (value M). Suppose we start at K in [L,R]. The maximum m is the highest barrier inside. If K != m, then to expand beyond m, the process must at some point absorb m. Before absorbing m, Takahashi's interval is entirely on K's side of m (left or right), because m blocks passage. Let S be the sum of the interval on that side that he has absorbed before m. He can absorb m iff S > M. What is the maximum S he can achieve on that side before m? It is exactly the final sum for starting K within the subinterval [L,m-1] (if K left) but with the boundary at m treated as an absorbing barrier? Actually within the subinterval, m is an outside neighbor of value M. The process on the left side is the same as the original problem restricted to [L,m-1] with an additional right boundary of height M. Maybe we can break ties in a way that equal values are not considered "maximum" for the purpose of absorption? Let's think.

The absorption condition is based solely on size. If we define a strict order where i < j if A_i < A_j, and for equal A_i = A_j, neither can absorb the other unless current sum > A_i. The recursive maximum argument relied on "after absorbing the maximum, current sum > all other elements in the interval". If there are equal maxima, after absorbing one maximum M, current sum = previous_sum + M. To absorb another equal M, need current sum > M, i.e., previous_sum > 0, which is true if previous_sum > M? Wait to absorb the first maximum M, we needed previous_sum > M. Thus after absorbing it, current sum > 2M > M, so equal maxima are absorbable! Therefore, if we can absorb one maximum of an interval, we can absorb all equal maxima and the entire interval. The problem is only if the starting K is a maximum and there is no smaller element accessible to make previous_sum > M before absorbing another equal maximum. But if we start at a maximum and there is any adjacent smaller element, we can grow and then absorb equals. If no adjacent smaller, we cannot.

In the Cartesian tree, a node's subtree may contain equal maxima. To absorb the subtree, starting at the node (which is one of the maxima), we need to be able to absorb at least one smaller element somewhere to grow. If the node has no smaller element in its entire subtree (i.e., all values in subtree equal A[node]), then it cannot absorb any other equal, so its reachable interval is just the singleton? What if the subtree has multiple equal nodes and no smaller, but the node is not at the boundary? Example A=[5,5,5], K=2 middle: neighbors equal5, cannot absorb, final [2,2]. K=1 final [1,1], K=3 [3,3]. So yes singleton.

If the subtree has smaller elements but they are behind equal barriers, can the node reach them? In a Cartesian tree with tie-breaking, the smaller elements are in subtrees of equal children. The node may not be adjacent to them. But perhaps if we choose tie-breaking appropriately (e.g., by index), the equal children are arranged so that a smaller element is adjacent to some equal maximum, and that equal maximum can absorb it, then the equal maximum can absorb other equals, etc. But starting at the root equal maximum, can it absorb an equal child that has a smaller element? It cannot absorb the equal child initially. However, maybe the equal child can absorb the smaller element first? But only Takahashi acts, not the equal child. So if the equal child is between K and the smaller element, K cannot access the smaller element. Thus K cannot grow. Example [5,5,1] K=1: equal child index2 blocks smaller index3. K=1 cannot grow. In the Cartesian tree root index1 has right child index2 (equal), whose right child index3 (smaller). The smaller is in the subtree but inaccessible. So the simple subtree sum is not reachable.

Therefore, for equal values, we need a different decomposition that treats equal maxima as barriers unless there is a path of strictly smaller elements adjacent to the source.

Maybe we can perturb equal values by infinitesimal amounts based on position to create a strict order, but the absorption condition should be A_i < current sum, not tie-break. If we assign infinitesimal eps to break ties, then a slime with slightly larger perturbed value could absorb equal original if current sum > original? Actually if we use perturbed values for comparison, we might allow absorbing equal when current sum == original? Need not. But maybe if we choose tie-break such that the "maximum" of an interval is the one that can absorb all others if any smaller exists? Let's examine.

Suppose we break ties by index: define value' = A_i + i*eps. Then for equal A, larger index is "larger". If we build a max Cartesian tree on value', then starting at a node, it can absorb descendants with smaller value' (which includes equal A but smaller index). But actual absorption of equal A requires current sum > A, not just > value'. If current sum > A, then it's allowed. If current sum == A, not allowed. The condition to climb using S_v > A[parent] (original) is correct. But the subtree of a node under value' may include equal A with smaller index that are not actually absorbable initially. However, if S_v > A[parent] is used for climbing, maybe the initial absorption of the node's own subtree is assumed. Is S_v reachable? This is wrong: we should not treat equal values as larger than smaller values. Tie-break should not make a smaller actual value an ancestor of a larger actual value. We need handle equal values as a special "plateau" where any of the equal maxima can be the root depending on source.

Maybe we can compress equal values? If there is a plateau of equal maximum values, the source can start at any position in the plateau. To absorb the plateau, it needs to absorb a smaller element adjacent to the plateau (if starting inside plateau) to grow. If starting at a plateau element with no smaller adjacent on either side within the interval, it cannot absorb other plateau elements. This is like the plateau acts as a single barrier of height M with multiple cells; absorbing one cell of height M requires sum > M. If you start on the plateau, you have initial sum M, not >M, so you cannot absorb adjacent plateau cells. But you can absorb a smaller neighbor if one is adjacent to your current cell. Once you absorb a smaller neighbor, sum > M, then you can absorb the rest of the plateau. So the reachability within a plateau depends on the position of the source and the positions of smaller adjacent cells.

This is getting complex. Let's see if we can avoid equal-value issues by a perturbation that preserves actual comparisons: We need a strict total order such that if A_i < A_j, then i is "smaller" regardless of perturbation; if A_i = A_j, tie-break can be arbitrary but should not affect absorption condition. The Cartesian tree should be built on actual values, with ties broken in a way that equal values are not ancestors of smaller values. That is, use key (A_i, tie_break) where tie_break can be index, but A_i primary. Then smaller actual values are always descendants of larger actual values. Equal values can be ordered by tie-break. Thus the "root" of a plateau should be the maximum that has access to a smaller element, if any; otherwise no one can absorb the plateau.

This suggests we need a more nuanced Cartesian tree for equal values: For a plateau of equal maxima, the interval can be absorbed from a starting position within the plateau only if there is a smaller element adjacent to the connected component of the source within the plateau? Actually starting at a plateau cell, you can absorb smaller neighbors immediately adjacent to your current cell. After absorbing one, sum > M, then you can absorb all adjacent plateau cells, and then continue. So the source can absorb the entire plateau if there exists at least one smaller element adjacent to the plateau on either side of the source's current position? More precisely, within the interval, the source at position K with value M. If there is any adjacent element (left or right) with value < M, absorb it, sum > M, then all other elements in the interval (including equal M) are < sum, so absorb all. If both immediate neighbors are >= M (i.e., equal M or larger), then cannot absorb any smaller neighbor; but if there is a smaller element further away, it is behind equal M neighbors, inaccessible. Thus for a node's subtree interval, starting at the node K (which is a maximum of that interval), K can absorb the entire subtree iff at least one of its immediate neighbors within the subtree has value < A[K]. If both immediate neighbors (within the interval) are equal to A[K] or non-existent? If neighbor equal, cannot. If neighbor larger, cannot be in subtree because K is maximum (by value) of subtree, so no larger. Thus condition: K has at least one adjacent index in the subtree with A < A[K]. If true, K can absorb the entire subtree. If false, all adjacent positions in the subtree have value equal to A[K] (or none), meaning the subtree is a contiguous block of equal values containing K, with no smaller elements adjacent to the block? Wait if subtree interval contains smaller elements not adjacent to K, they are separated by equal elements. K=3 has left neighbor equal, no smaller adjacent, so cannot absorb subtree. So condition is immediate neighbor smaller.

If K has an immediate smaller neighbor within the subtree, then K can absorb that neighbor, sum > M, then absorb all equal neighbors and the rest of the subtree (since all values <= M < sum). Thus the entire subtree sum is reachable. If not, K cannot absorb any equal neighbor, so its reachable interval is just the singleton? Therefore, for a node in the Cartesian tree (with tie-breaking), the "absorbable sum" from that node may be either S_v (if v has an adjacent smaller element within its subtree) or A[v] (if not). But after climbing to a parent, the current sum is S_parent (if we absorbed the whole parent subtree). The condition to climb from parent to grandparent is S_parent > A[grandparent]. This remains valid if S_parent is actually reachable. If a node's own subtree is not fully reachable from the starting K, we wouldn't be at that node.

Thus we need compute for each starting index K the highest ancestor u such that along the path, each node's subtree sum is reachable and the climb condition holds. The reachability of a node's subtree from its root (the node) depends on whether the root has an adjacent smaller element within its subtree. Once we climb into a parent, the parent's subtree is fully absorbed because we crossed the parent maximum with sufficient sum. So the algorithm with tie-breaking and an initial reachability check for the starting node might work:
- Build a Cartesian tree using key (A_i, tie_break) where tie_break is consistent (e.g., index). This gives each node v a subtree interval I_v and sum S_v.
- For each node v, define base_reachable[v] = true if starting at index v, Takahashi can absorb the entire subtree I_v. This is true iff v has at least one neighbor within I_v with A < A[v]. (If I_v size 1, false? Starting at singleton, it already is the subtree; final size A[v], which equals S_v. Absorbing the entire subtree of size1 is trivially true. For climbing, S_v = A[v]. If parent exists equal, condition S_v > A[parent] false. So size1 should be considered reachable. The condition "neighbor smaller" is not needed because no absorption needed. So base_reachable[v] = True if subtree size == 1 or (L_v < v and A[L_v? wait immediate neighbor in interval) or (v < R_v and A[v+1] < A[v])? Need immediate left/right neighbor within the subtree. Since the subtree interval is contiguous, the immediate neighbors of v within the interval are v-1 (if L_v <= v-1) and v+1 (if v+1 <= R_v). If either has A < A[v], then v can absorb it and then the whole subtree. If both neighbors (within interval) have A == A[v] or absent, and subtree size >1, then v cannot absorb any neighbor, so cannot absorb the subtree. What if one neighbor has A > A[v]? Impossible because v is maximum of subtree by key; but if tie-break, equal values can be in subtree, larger values cannot. So only equal or smaller.
- For nodes reached by climbing, we don't need base_reachable because the climb condition ensures we have enough sum to absorb the parent. But what if the child subtree was not fully reachable? We only climb from a node that is reachable. We need propagate reachability through the tree: A node v is "reachable as a final interval" for starting indices in its subtree? Actually for a starting index K, the path starts at node K. We need know if node K can absorb its own subtree. If not, final is singleton K (not even subtree? Wait if K cannot absorb its subtree, it might still absorb a smaller neighbor if one exists? But base_reachable false means no immediate smaller neighbor within subtree. If no immediate smaller neighbor, it cannot absorb anything, so final singleton. If there is an immediate smaller neighbor, base_reachable true and it absorbs entire subtree. So yes.)
- If base_reachable[K] is false, final interval is just [K,K]? But what if K has a smaller neighbor outside its Cartesian subtree? The Cartesian subtree of K is the maximal interval where K is the maximum. Outside, there is a larger or equal element (parent) adjacent to the subtree. If K has no smaller neighbor inside, its immediate neighbors inside are equal or none. Its immediate neighbor outside could be smaller? If outside is smaller, then K would be maximum of a larger interval including it, contradiction. So outside neighbor is >= A[K]. Thus cannot absorb. So final singleton.
- If base_reachable[K] is true, then K can absorb its subtree I_K, sum S_K. Then it can climb to parent p if S_K > A[p]. If yes, absorb parent and entire I_p. Now at node p, do we need base_reachable[p]? No, because we already have sum S_K > A[p], so after absorbing p, current sum = S_K + A[p] > 2A[p] >= all elements in I_p (since A[p] is max by value; equal elements also < 2A[p]? If equal to A[p], current sum > 2A[p] > A[p], so absorbable). Thus the entire I_p is absorbed regardless of p's original neighbors. So the climb condition is simply S_child > A[parent] for the child node we are coming from. Note S_child is the sum of the child's subtree, which is reachable. After climbing to parent, the new current sum is S_parent (sum of parent's subtree). Then continue with condition S_parent > A[grandparent].

Thus the process for starting K:
1. Let v = K (node index).
2. If base_reachable[v] is false: final node = v? But final interval should be singleton [K,K], not necessarily I_v. Wait if base_reachable false, K cannot absorb its subtree, but its subtree may be larger than singleton (e.g., [1,5,5] K=3, subtree [1,3] size3). Final interval is [3,3], not I_v. So we cannot just set final node = v with S_v. We need a special "singleton" node representing just K. In the Cartesian tree, node v's subtree is not reachable, so the final interval is the singleton {v}, not the node's subtree. But the node v itself in the tree represents the index; its subtree interval may be larger. If base_reachable false, the final interval is [v,v] with sum A[v]. It cannot climb to parent because it hasn't absorbed the subtree. So we should treat the starting "current interval" as the singleton node v with sum A[v], not the full subtree. Then to climb, the condition to absorb parent would be A[v] > A[parent]? But parent has value >= A[v] (by Cartesian tree), so false. Thus final singleton. So we can handle by: if base_reachable[v] false, answer = A[v].
3. If base_reachable[v] true, current node = v, current sum = S_v. Then climb while parent p exists and current sum > A[p]. When we climb, current node becomes p, current sum = S_p. Continue. Final answer = S_final_node.

But wait: If base_reachable[v] true, K absorbs its entire subtree I_v. What if I_v is just singleton? base_reachable true, S_v=A[v]. Then climb condition A[v] > A[parent]? Since parent value >= A[v], false, so final singleton. Good.

Now we need build a Cartesian tree with tie-breaking such that each node's subtree is the maximal interval where it is the maximum according to (A, tie). The base_reachable condition uses immediate neighbors within the subtree and actual A values. Does tie-breaking affect the subtree intervals and thus base_reachable? We need choose a tie-breaking that makes the recursive climb valid. For equal values, the Cartesian tree with any tie-break (e.g., by index) will arrange equal values in a chain. Node3 [3,3] S5 parent idx2 val5 cond 5>5 false -> final5. Actual K=3 final [3,3] yes.
Works.

Root idx1, right child idx2.

Node2 [2,2] S5 parent idx1 val5 cond false -> final5. Actual K2 final [2,2].
Works.

So any tie-break seems to work? Need test a case where tie-break affects base_reachable and climb. Consider [5,1,5,5]? Let's pick tie-break larger index. A: idx1=5, idx2=1, idx3=5, idx4=5.
Max of [1,4] is idx4 (5,4) vs idx3 (5,3) vs idx1; root idx4. Left interval [1,3] max idx3 (5,3), left child idx1, right child idx2? Actually interval [1,2] max idx1, right child idx2. So tree: root4, left child3, left child of3=1, right child of3=2.

Tie-break larger index worked.

Now, is the condition for base_reachable[v] simply "there exists an immediate neighbor within I_v with A < A[v]"? What if the immediate neighbor has A > A[v]? Not possible because v is max of I_v by key (A, tie). If equal by A but lower tie, it's not < A[v]. So only smaller.

If base_reachable[v] is false, final answer is A[v] (singleton). But is it possible that v has no immediate smaller neighbor in I_v, but has an immediate smaller neighbor outside I_v that is not separated by an equal/larger element? Since I_v is the maximal interval where v is maximum (by key). Outside I_v, the adjacent element (if any) must have key greater than v, meaning A > A[v] or A == A[v] and tie higher. Thus not smaller. So no.

If base_reachable[v] is true, K absorbs I_v. Then climb condition uses S_v > A[parent]. What if parent value equals A[v] and S_v > A[parent]? Since S_v includes A[v] plus other positive elements, if I_v size >1, S_v > A[v] = A[parent], so condition true. This corresponds to absorbing equal parent after growing on smaller neighbor. If I_v size=1, S_v = A[v] = A[parent], condition false, so cannot absorb equal parent. Good.

Now, after climbing to parent p, we set current sum = S_p. Is S_p always reachable? We had current sum before absorbing p = S_v > A[p]. After absorbing p, sum = S_v + A[p]. But S_p = S_v + A[p] + sum(other child subtrees). Are the other child subtrees absorbable? All elements in other child subtrees have value <= A[p] (since p is max of I_p). Current sum = S_v + A[p] > 2A[p] (because S_v > A[p]). Thus current sum > 2A[p] >= 2 * any element in other child subtrees? If an element equals A[p], current sum > 2A[p] > A[p], so absorbable. Thus yes, all other elements are smaller than current sum, so can be absorbed one by one regardless of order. Therefore S_p reachable. Good.

Thus the algorithm reduces to:
- Build a Cartesian tree over indices 1..N with comparator: i < j if A_i < A_j, or A_i == A_j and some tie-break (e.g., i < j for smaller index? Need choose). The root of any interval is the index with maximum A, tie-break by index (say larger index has higher priority, or smaller; either works? Need ensure parent value >= child value by A. Yes.)
- For each node v, compute L[v], R[v] (subtree interval), S[v] = sum A[L..R].
- Compute parent[ v ].
- Compute base_reachable[v]:
  if L[v] == R[v]: True
  else:
    ok = False
    if v > L[v] and A[v-1] < A[v]: ok = True  # left neighbor inside interval
    if v < R[v] and A[v+1] < A[v]: ok = True  # right neighbor inside interval
    base_reachable[v] = ok
- For each starting index K (which is a node), if base_reachable[K] is False, answer = A[K].
  Else, we need to climb from K while S[current] > A[parent[current]]. But note if base_reachable[K] True, current sum = S[K]. We can use binary lifting to find final node. The condition to move from node v to parent p is S[v] > A[p]. This condition depends on S[v] and A[p]. For a path, after moving to p, the condition for next move uses S[p], not S[v]. So we need binary lifting over "can climb 2^k edges from v" where each edge condition uses the S of the node at the start of each edge. Define can[v][0] = (parent[v] exists and S[v] > A[parent[v]]). For k>0, can[v][k] = can[v][k-1] and can[ up[k-1][v] ][k-1], where up[k-1][v] is the ancestor after 2^{k-1} climbs. But careful: after climbing 2^{k-1} edges, we are at node mid = ancestor after 2^{k-1} edges. The conditions for the next 2^{k-1} edges are exactly can[mid][k-1]. So yes.
  Then starting from v=K, for k descending, if can[v][k], v = up[k][v]. After loop, v is the highest reachable node (where can[v][0] false or root). Answer = S[v].
But wait: If base_reachable[K] is True, we start at K with sum S[K]. The can[K][0] condition uses S[K] > A[parent]. Good. If base_reachable[K] is False, we should not use S[K] because S[K] is not reachable; answer A[K]. So we handle separately.

Need ensure that for nodes with base_reachable False, can[v][0] might be true if S[v] > A[parent] (e.g., [5,5] node2? base false, S=10, parent none; no. [1,5,5] node3 base false, S=11, parent none. Could there be base false with parent and S[v] > A[parent]? Example [5,5,1]? Node1 base true. Need find base false but S[v] > parent. Suppose [1,5,5] node3 base false, root no parent. If a base false node has parent, its subtree is a plateau of equal values with no smaller immediate neighbor, so S[v] = size * M. Parent value >= M. If parent value = M (equal), S[v] > M if size>1, so can[v][0] would be true, but starting at v cannot absorb its own subtree, so should not climb. We handle by not using can for base false. Good.

Now, is base_reachable[K] True sufficient for K to absorb its entire subtree I_K? Let's prove:
- If I_K size 1, trivial.
- If there is an immediate neighbor within I_K with A < A[K], K absorbs it, sum becomes A[K] + smaller > A[K]. Now all other elements in I_K have A <= A[K] < sum (strictly, since sum > A[K]). Therefore K can absorb all remaining elements one by one, regardless of adjacency? They are in the same interval; after absorbing the smaller neighbor, the interval expands. Any remaining element has value < sum, and is adjacent to the current interval eventually? Since the interval is contiguous and contains K; to absorb an element on the far side, it will become adjacent after intermediate elements are absorbed. All intermediate elements also have value <= A[K] < sum, so they can be absorbed. Thus yes.
- If no immediate smaller neighbor, all immediate neighbors in I_K (if any) have A == A[K] (cannot be larger). K cannot absorb any neighbor, so it cannot leave singleton. Thus base false.

Now, building the Cartesian tree with tie-break. We need compute parent, left child, right child, and subtree intervals. Standard O(N) monotonic stack for Cartesian tree (max-heap) with comparator. Since N up to 5e5, recursion depth could be O(N), so build iteratively and compute subtree intervals via post-order or using stack.

Standard algorithm for Cartesian tree (max):
For i from 1 to N:
  last = 0
  while stack not empty and A[stack[-1]] < A[i] (or comparator: stack top has lower priority than i):
      last = stack.pop()
  if stack not empty:
      right[stack[-1]] = i
      parent[i] = stack[-1]
  if last != 0:
      left[i] = last
      parent[last] = i
  stack.append(i)

This builds a Cartesian tree where the priority is A (max) with tie-breaking? The condition A[stack[-1]] < A[i] means if equal, not popped, so earlier equal remains ancestor (tie-break smaller index? Actually if equal, stack top stays, so i becomes right child of earlier equal. Thus earlier index has higher priority. So tie-break: smaller index is higher priority (root is leftmost maximum). If we want larger index higher priority, use <=? Need be consistent. Any tie-break works as tested. Let's choose a tie-break and implement base_reachable accordingly. The standard algorithm with `<` gives earlier equal as ancestor (leftmost max). We tested tie-break smaller index works. Let's use that (A[stack[-1]] < A[i] pops only strictly smaller, so equal not popped; earlier equal higher priority). Then parent value >= child value. Good.

Need compute subtree intervals L[v], R[v]. We can compute after building children. Since each node's subtree interval is contiguous. We can do iterative post-order DFS from root(s) to compute L,R,S. Or we can compute L,R using the fact that in Cartesian tree, L[v] is the leftmost index in its subtree, R[v] rightmost. We can compute by traversing children. N 5e5, recursion limit maybe set high but iterative safer.

We also need prefix sums for S[v]. Since subtree interval contiguous, S[v] = pref[R[v]] - pref[L[v]-1]. So we just need L,R.

Compute L,R:
Initialize L[i]=R[i]=i.
Process nodes in reverse order? In Cartesian tree, children have indices less/greater? Not necessarily. But we can do a post-order traversal using stack:
For each root (parent=0), push. Or simply iterate i from 1..N? Since parent of i can be < or > i. Need DFS.

We can compute L,R by:
For i from 1 to N:
  if left[i]: L[i] = L[left[i]]? But left subtree may not be processed if left child index > i? In Cartesian tree, left child index < i, right child index > i? Is that always? The Cartesian tree built with in-order indices: left child is in left subarray, so left child index < parent; right child index > parent. Yes, because the tree preserves inorder (indices). Thus left child index < i, right child index > i. So we can compute L[i] by processing i increasing? For node i, L[i] = L[left[i]] if left child exists else i. Since left child index < i, L[left] already computed if we process i increasing. R[i] = R[right[i]] if right child exists else i, but right child index > i, not computed yet if increasing. We can process i decreasing for R. Or do one pass with post-order.

Simpler: Since left child index < i and right child index > i, we can compute L[i] in increasing order:
L[i] = L[left[i]] if left[i] else i.
Then compute R[i] in decreasing order:
R[i] = R[right[i]] if right[i] else i.
Let's verify: For node i, its subtree interval spans from L of left child (if any) to R of right child (if any). Since left child index < i, its L already computed when increasing. Right child index > i, its R computed when decreasing. This works! Need ensure left child's R not needed for L; right child's L not needed for R. The interval is [L[left] or i, R[right] or i]. Yes.

Then S[v] = pref[R[v]] - pref[L[v]-1].

Base_reachable:
For node v:
if L[v] == R[v]: True
else:
  ok = False
  if v > L[v] and A[v-1] < A[v]: ok = True
  if v < R[v] and A[v+1] < A[v]: ok = True
  base[v] = ok
But wait: What if the immediate neighbor within the subtree is not v-1/v+1 because the subtree interval may have gaps? No, subtree interval is contiguous, so if v > L[v], index v-1 is within [L[v], R[v]] (since L[v] <= v-1). Similarly v+1. Good.

Now binary lifting:
LOG = ceil(log2(N)) + 1.
up[0][v] = parent[v] (0 for root).
can[0][v] = (parent[v] != 0 and S[v] > A[parent[v]]). But this is only valid if v's subtree is reachable. For base false nodes, we won't start from them, but they could be mid in a jump? Can a base false node be reached as mid when climbing from a descendant? Wait if we start at K with base true, we climb to ancestors. The nodes we pass through are ancestors of K. Could an ancestor have base false? Yes, e.g., [1,5,5] K=2 starts at node2 base true, climbs to parent node3 which is root and base false. But we don't need base of node3 to climb into it; we already climbed using can[0][node2] = S_node2 > A[parent]. After moving to node3, can[0][node3] would be false (root no parent). The binary lifting can[0][mid] for mid=node3 is false, which is correct for further climbing. But what if mid is base false and has a parent, and can[0][mid] would be true based on S[mid] > A[parent], but since we arrived at mid by climbing, we have absorbed the entire subtree of mid (S[mid]) even if base[mid] false. So can[0][mid] should be valid for further climbing! Wait, if we climbed into a base false node, we did absorb its entire subtree (because we crossed its max with enough sum). Thus S[mid] is reachable, and can[0][mid] should be based on S[mid] > A[parent[mid]], regardless of base[mid]. So base_reachable is only needed for the starting node, not for nodes reached by climbing. Therefore can[0][v] should be defined for all v as (parent exists and S[v] > A[parent])? But if v is base false and we start at v, we shouldn't use can. However, can[v] might be true and binary lifting from v would incorrectly climb. We handle starting base false separately. For base true starts, can for all nodes along path is valid because once reached, S is reachable. But what if a base false node is not on the path from a base true start? We won't use it as mid because we never start there. In binary lifting, when climbing from a base true start, the mids are ancestors. They are reached by valid climbs, so their S is reachable. Thus can[0] based on S is valid for them. So we can define can[0][v] = (parent[v] != 0 and S[v] > A[parent[v]]) for all v. For a base false start, we bypass and answer A[v]. For a base true start, use can.

But wait: What if a base true start climbs to a base false node mid, and can[mid][0] is true (S[mid] > A[parent[mid]]), then we would climb further. Is that valid? Example [1,5,5] K=2: node2 base true, S=6, parent node3 val5, can[0][2]=true. Move to node3 (base false, root no parent). Can a base false node be an ancestor of a base true start? Suppose start in a subtree that is a child of a base false node. To climb into the base false node, we need S_child > A[base_false]. If true, we absorb the base false node and its entire subtree. Then the base false node becomes current. It may have a parent; can[base_false][0] based on S[base_false] is valid. Example [1,5,5] with K=2: node2 base true, parent node3 base false root. No further. Add parent: [1,5,5,6]? Node3 base false is not ancestor of node2; node2's parent is root6. Need a base false node that is ancestor of a base true node. Tie-break smaller index: base false nodes are rightmost equal maxima in a plateau with no smaller immediate neighbor. They tend to be right children of the leftmost max? In [1,5,5], node3 base false is right child of node2, so descendant, not ancestor. In [5,5,1], tie-break smaller index: root idx1, right child idx2, right child idx3. Node1 base false? Wait [5,5,1] with smaller index: root idx1 (5), right child idx2 (5), right child idx3 (1). Good.

Therefore we can precompute can[0][v] for all v as (parent[v] != 0 and S[v] > A[parent[v]]). For starting K with base_reachable[K] false, we should not climb; answer A[K]. For base true, we use binary lifting with can.

But wait: What if base_reachable[K] true but S[K] is not the sum of the subtree because the subtree contains equal maxima that are not absorbable? We argued base true implies S[K] reachable. Need ensure with tie-break smaller index, if K has an immediate smaller neighbor within I_K, then it can absorb the entire I_K. We proved. Good.

Now, is it possible that base_reachable[K] false but K can absorb some elements outside its subtree before absorbing inside? We argued outside neighbor has A >= A[K] (because I_K is maximal interval where K is max by key; outside parent has key > K, so A >= A[K]). If A > A[K], cannot absorb. If A == A[K] and tie higher, cannot absorb because equal. So no.

Thus the algorithm seems solid.

Great.

Now, one more subtlety: The Cartesian tree built with tie-break smaller index gives parent value >= child value. The climb condition S[v] > A[parent] uses S[v] = sum of v's subtree. But if v's subtree contains elements with value equal to parent and parent is not the maximum? Parent is maximum of its interval, so all elements in v's subtree have value <= A[parent]. If some equal, current sum S[v] might be > A[parent] even if v's subtree is just one equal element plus smaller? Actually if S[v] > A[parent], then after absorbing parent, current sum S[v] + A[parent] > 2A[parent], so all elements in parent's other subtrees (<=A[parent]) are absorbable. Good.

Need prove the recursive climb property for equal values with tie-break:
Let p be parent of v in Cartesian tree. I_v is a child subtree of p. Suppose starting from some K in I_v, we have absorbed all of I_v, sum S_v, and S_v > A[p]. Then we can absorb p. After absorbing p, can we absorb the entire I_p? I_p consists of p, I_v, and possibly the other child subtree(s) (at most two children in Cartesian tree: left and right). The other child subtree is on the other side of p. All elements in it have value <= A[p] (since p is max of I_p by key; equal allowed). Current sum = S_v + A[p] > 2A[p]. Thus every element in the other subtree has value < current sum (strictly, because <= A[p] < 2A[p] < current sum). Therefore they can be absorbed one by one. Thus S_p reachable. Good.

Now, base_reachable for initial node K:
If I_K has an immediate neighbor with value < A[K], K absorbs it, sum > A[K]. Then all other elements in I_K have value <= A[K] < sum, so absorbable. If no immediate smaller neighbor, K cannot move. What if I_K has size >1 and the immediate neighbor is equal, but K could absorb a smaller element on the other side of that equal neighbor? No, equal neighbor blocks. So false.

Thus algorithm is correct.

Now implement.

Steps in code:

1. Read N, A (1-indexed).
2. Build Cartesian tree:
   parent = [0]*(N+1)
   left = [0]*(N+1)
   right = [0]*(N+1)
   stack = []
   for i in 1..N:
       last = 0
       while stack and A[stack[-1]] < A[i]:  # tie-break smaller index: equal not popped
           last = stack.pop()
       if stack:
           right[stack[-1]] = i
           parent[i] = stack[-1]
       if last:
           left[i] = last
           parent[last] = i
       stack.append(i)
   root = stack[0]? Actually after processing, stack contains roots? In Cartesian tree, there is one root (the maximum of whole array) because we process all; stack bottom is root. With tie-break smaller index, root is leftmost maximum. parent[root]=0. The stack may contain a chain? At end, stack[0] is root. But parent already set. We don't need root separately for binary lifting, but for L,R we can use children.

Need verify parent assignments with standard algorithm. Let's test [2,1,3]:
i1 stack [1]
i2: A2=1 < A1=2? no. stack top1, right[1]=2, parent[2]=1, stack [1,2].
i3: A3=3 > A2=1 pop last=2; A3=3 > A1=2 pop last=1. stack empty. if last: left[3]=1? Wait last after pops is 1 (last popped). left[3]=1, parent[1]=3. But what about node2? It was right child of1, and since 1 is left child of3, node2 is in left subtree of3 via 1. Good. parent[2] remains1. stack [3]. Tree root3 left1 right? right[1]=2. Correct.

Need ensure when popping, the last popped is the highest (closest to i) or the last? In standard algorithm, last is the last popped, which becomes left child of i. The popped nodes form the right spine; the last popped is the topmost of the popped subtree, which should be left child of i. Yes.

But there is a known issue: When setting right[stack[-1]] = i, if stack[-1] already had a right child that was popped, that child is now in last and will be set as left[i], so overwriting right[stack[-1]] is correct. Good.

3. Compute L,R:
   L = [0]*(N+1); R=[0]*(N+1)
   for i in 1..N:
       if left[i]: L[i] = L[left[i]]
       else: L[i] = i
   for i in range(N,0,-1):
       if right[i]: R[i] = R[right[i]]
       else: R[i] = i
   But wait: For a node with both left and right children, L[i] = L[left[i]] (leftmost of left subtree), R[i] = R[right[i]] (rightmost of right subtree). If no left child, L[i]=i; if no right child, R[i]=i. This works because left child index < i, right child index > i. Need ensure L[left[i]] computed when processing increasing: left child index < i, yes. R[right[i]] computed when processing decreasing: right child index > i, yes. Good.

   Let's test [2,1,3]: left[3]=1, L1=1, L3=1. R: right[1]=2, R2=2, R1=2; R3=3. Node1 interval [1,2], node3 [1,3]. Correct.

4. Prefix sums pref[0..N].
   S[v] = pref[R[v]] - pref[L[v]-1].

5. base_reachable:
   base = [False]*(N+1)
   for i in 1..N:
       if L[i] == R[i]:
           base[i] = True
       else:
           if i > L[i] and A[i-1] < A[i]:
               base[i] = True
           elif i < R[i] and A[i+1] < A[i]:
               base[i] = True
   Is it possible that both neighbors are outside but L<R? If L<R and i is at boundary, only one neighbor inside. Good.

6. Binary lifting:
   LOG = (N).bit_length()
   up = [[0]*(N+1) for _ in range(LOG)]
   can = [[False]*(N+1) for _ in range(LOG)]
   for i in 1..N:
       up[0][i] = parent[i]
       can[0][i] = (parent[i] != 0 and S[i] > A[parent[i]])
   For k in 1..LOG-1:
       for i in 1..N:
           mid = up[k-1][i]
           if mid != 0:
               up[k][i] = up[k-1][mid]
               can[k][i] = can[k-1][i] and can[k-1][mid]
           else:
               up[k][i] = 0
               can[k][i] = False
   Note: can[k][i] should mean starting at node i, can climb 2^k edges. If can[k-1][i] true, we move to mid = up[k-1][i] (after 2^{k-1} edges). Then need can[k-1][mid] to climb another 2^{k-1}. If mid=0, can false. Good.

7. For each K 1..N:
   if not base[K]:
       ans = A[K]
   else:
       v = K
       for k in reversed(range(LOG)):
           if can[k][v]:
               v = up[k][v]
       ans = S[v]
   Collect ans.

Need verify binary lifting loop: Starting at v, for k desc, if can[k][v] true, v = up[k][v]. This climbs as far as possible. But after moving, we continue with lower k. Standard. At the end, v is the highest node reachable (where can[0][v] false or root). Let's test with chain increasing [1,2,3,4,5]:

But we can also avoid storing can table by computing a "jump" table where we store the highest reachable ancestor directly? Maybe we can compute final node for all base true nodes using DP on tree? Let's explore.

The climb process: final[v] = final[parent[v]] if S[v] > A[parent[v]] else v? Wait if cond(v) true, we move to parent p. Then final[v] = final[p] (because from p we continue). If cond(v) false, final[v] = v. This is a simple recurrence! Is that true? Starting at v, if can climb to parent p, then the final node from v is the same as the final node starting from p (with the assumption that p's subtree is reachable). But final[p] as computed by the recurrence for a start at p might use base_reachable[p]? We need be careful: final[p] in the recurrence should represent the final node when starting at p with its subtree already reachable? Actually if we start at p from scratch, we might not be able to absorb its subtree (base false). But when we arrive at p by climbing, its subtree is reachable. So we need a separate DP for "arrived at node with subtree reachable". Let's define climb_final[v] = the highest node reachable when starting at node v with the assumption that the entire subtree of v has already been absorbed (so current sum = S[v]). Then recurrence:
if parent[v] exists and S[v] > A[parent[v]]:
    climb_final[v] = climb_final[parent[v]]
else:
    climb_final[v] = v
Because after climbing to parent, we have absorbed parent's entire subtree, so we are in the same situation as "arrived at parent with subtree absorbed". This recurrence is simple and can be computed by processing nodes from root down? climb_final[v] depends on climb_final[parent], so process nodes in order from root to leaves (top-down). We can compute for all v in O(N) without binary lifting! Is this valid? Let's test.

For sample:

This recurrence works because the climb condition only depends on the current node's subtree sum S[v], not on the starting K. Once you are at v with its subtree absorbed, the future is independent of how you got there. Therefore we can compute `jump[v]` = final node from v in the "subtree absorbed" state:
jump[v] = jump[parent[v]] if parent[v] and S[v] > A[parent[v]] else v.

We can compute this top-down from root to leaves. Since parent index can be less or greater? In Cartesian tree, parent can be less or greater index. We need process nodes in an order such that parent before child. We can do a BFS/DFS from root, or since parent index may be any, use stack. But we can also compute recursively with iterative stack from root. Or use the fact that the tree is a Cartesian tree; parent relation not ordered by index. We can find root(s) and do DFS.

But wait: Does jump[v] depend on parent's jump, which may depend on grandparent, etc. So top-down from root works. We need root. There is exactly one root for the whole array (the maximum with tie-break smaller index). Because Cartesian tree over all N has one root. So we can start from root and traverse children.

We can compute jump for all nodes by:
- Initialize jump[root]=root.
- Traverse tree from root (stack) in preorder; for child c of v:
    if S[c] > A[v]: jump[c] = jump[v]  # because parent of c is v, and cond true
    else: jump[c] = c
Wait parent of c is v. Condition to climb from c to v is S[c] > A[v]. If true, then final from c = jump[v]. If false, final = c. This is exactly recurrence. This avoids binary lifting entirely! Great.

Traverse:
jump = [0]*(N+1)
jump[root] = root
stack = [root]
while stack:
    v = stack.pop()
    # for children
    if left[v]:
        c = left[v]
        if S[c] > A[v]:
            jump[c] = jump[v]
        else:
            jump[c] = c
        stack.append(c)
    if right[v]:
        c = right[v]
        if S[c] > A[v]:
            jump[c] = jump[v]
        else:
            jump[c] = c
        stack.append(c)

This computes jump for all nodes. Need ensure if a node has parent p, the condition uses A[p] (parent value). In traversal, when at v, children's parent is v, so condition S[child] > A[v]. Correct.

Now, we should prove jump recurrence:
For node v in "subtree absorbed" state, current sum S[v]. If parent p exists and S[v] > A[p], then we can absorb p and entire I_p, reaching state at p with sum S[p]. From there, final is jump[p]. If not, cannot absorb p (or no parent), so final is v. Thus jump[v] = jump[p] if cond else v. This is independent of base. Good.

Now, let's think about the initial absorption of I_K when base true. We said K absorbs an immediate smaller neighbor, sum > A[K], then absorbs all I_K. But what if the immediate smaller neighbor is not adjacent to K because the subtree interval includes it but there is an equal neighbor between? Wait immediate neighbor means index K-1 or K+1. If K-1 is in I_K and A[K-1] < A[K], it's adjacent. If K-1 not in I_K, then the left boundary of I_K is K (no left inside). So the only possible immediate smaller neighbors are exactly K-1 or K+1 if they are inside I_K. We check that. Good.

Now, let's consider if base[K] true but K has an immediate smaller neighbor, but that neighbor is actually outside I_K? We check inside only. If outside smaller, impossible as argued. But our check uses L/R, so safe.

Now, memory and implementation details.

Building Cartesian tree with list of ints:
N up to 5e5, A list length N+1. parent, left, right lists of ints length N+1. Python int lists memory ~ 3*(N+1)*8 references + int objects? The ints are objects; but when assigning indices, each int object may be shared? Actually integers >256 are created; list stores references to int objects. parent[i]=stack[-1] references existing int object from stack? Stack stores int objects. Many int objects. Memory could be high but still maybe okay: 4 arrays of 500k ints ~ 4*500k*28? Wait list of ints stores references (8 bytes) plus int objects (28 bytes) for each distinct int. But when we assign `parent[i] = stack[-1]`, the int object is the same as in stack? stack contains int objects; parent references same object, no new int. However, `i` loop creates int objects? Python range yields ints; list stores references. The total number of int objects for indices 1..N might be N (from range) but many references. Memory for 500k ints ~14MB plus list refs 4MB per list. 4 lists ~72MB. A list of 500k large ints ~18MB? Actually A values up to 1e9, each int object 28 bytes + list refs 4MB -> 18MB. Prefix list another 18MB. Total maybe 150MB. Acceptable if memory 512MB. But we can reduce by using arrays? Not necessary maybe. But Python memory limit in AtCoder often 1024 MB for such problems? Could be 512 MB. We should be mindful.

We can use `array('i')` for parent, left, right, L, R to save memory. But list of ints is faster. N=5e5, 4 lists of ints ~ 4*(500k*8 + 500k*28?) Actually each element in a list is a reference to a PyLong object. If we create a list `[0]*(N+1)`, all zeros reference the same small int 0 (interned), so initially only list refs 8*(N+1) ~4MB per list. As we assign distinct ints, those int objects already exist? `stack[-1]` returns a PyLong object stored in stack. Stack stores references to PyLong objects created by range? Range iteration creates new PyLong for each i? It may reuse? Python creates a new int object for each value >256 when iterating range? Actually range iterator returns PyLong objects; when appended to stack, the object is kept. So for each index i, there is a PyLong object in stack at some point, and references in parent/left/right to those objects. But when popped, the object may still be referenced by parent/left/right. So eventually each index may have one PyLong object referenced by multiple lists. So total int objects ~N, 14MB, plus list refs 4 arrays*4MB=16MB. Not too bad. A values are separate PyLongs ~14MB. Prefix sums ~N PyLongs ~14MB + refs 4MB. Total maybe <150MB. Good.

We can also avoid storing left/right after computing L,R,jump? We need left/right for DFS. We can keep.

Now, prefix sums: pref list of ints length N+1. Compute:
pref = [0]*(N+1)
for i in range(1,N+1): pref[i]=pref[i-1]+A[i]
S[v] = pref[R[v]] - pref[L[v]-1]. We can compute S list length N+1.

Base list: could be bytearray of length N+1 for memory.

Jump list: list of ints length N+1.

Now, let's double-check the Cartesian tree build with tie-break smaller index and parent assignment for equal values. Standard algorithm with `while stack and A[stack[-1]] < A[i]` means equal values are not popped, so earlier equal becomes ancestor. This gives root as leftmost maximum. We tested. Need ensure for equal values, the subtree intervals are correct with L/R increasing/decreasing. Example [5,5,1] smaller index:
Build:
