Now, let's think about the problem statement: Takahashi is the K-th slime in the initial state. He can absorb adjacent slime strictly smaller than him. If he absorbs a slime, his size increases. The final maximum size is what we compute. We assumed he always absorbs entire subtree if base true. Could there be a strategy where he doesn't absorb an available smaller neighbor and instead absorbs something else to get a larger final size? Since all absorptions increase size and never hurt, absorbing any available slime is always beneficial. If base true, absorbing the immediate smaller neighbor is possible; doing so leads to absorbing all of I_K. Could there be a larger final interval than jump[K] by not absorbing I_K fully and instead doing something else? To reach outside I_K, he must absorb the parent, which requires current sum > A[parent]. The maximum sum he can have before absorbing parent is at most S[K] (if he absorbs all of I_K). If S[K] <= A[parent], he cannot absorb parent. If S[K] > A[parent], then absorbing all of I_K is possible and gives the largest pre-parent sum, so it maximizes chance to climb. Since absorbing more never hurts, the greedy maximal process yields the maximum. Our jump uses S[K] as pre-parent sum, which is the maximum possible before parent. Thus correct.

Now, let's ensure base true implies S[K] is reachable before parent. We proved.

Let's write code carefully.

Pseudo-code:

import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = [0] + list(map(int, input().split()))
    parent = [0]*(N+1)
    left = [0]*(N+1)
    right = [0]*(N+1)
    st = []
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        last = 0
        ai = A[i]
        while st and A[st[-1]] < ai:
            last = st.pop()
        if st:
            right[st[-1]] = i
            parent[i] = st[-1]
        if last:
            left[i] = last
            parent[last] = i
        st.append(i)
    root = st[0]

    L = [0]*(N+1)
    R = [0]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        if left[i]:
            L[i] = L[left[i]]
        else:
            L[i] = i
    for i in range(N, 0, -1):
        if right[i]:
            R[i] = R[right[i]]
        else:
            R[i] = i

    pref = [0]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        pref[i] = pref[i-1] + A[i]
    S = [0]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        S[i] = pref[R[i]] - pref[L[i]-1]

    base = bytearray(N+1)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        if L[i] == R[i]:
            base[i] = 1
        else:
            if i > L[i] and A[i-1] < A[i]:
                base[i] = 1
            elif i < R[i] and A[i+1] < A[i]:
                base[i] = 1

    jump = [0]*(N+1)
    jump[root] = root
    stack = [root]
    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        av = A[v]
        c = left[v]
        if c:
            if S[c] > av:
                jump[c] = jump[v]
            else:
                jump[c] = c
            stack.append(c)
        c = right[v]
        if c:
            if S[c] > av:
                jump[c] = jump[v]
            else:
                jump[c] = c
            stack.append(c)

    ans = []
    for k in range(1, N+1):
        if base[k]:
            ans.append(str(S[jump[k]]))
        else:
            ans.append(str(A[k]))
    print(' '.join(ans))

Need check `jump[v]` for children when `jump[v]` might not be computed? We process parent before child via stack. Since we push children after setting jump, yes. For root, jump[root]=root. For a child c, jump[c] uses jump[v] (parent) which is set. Good.

But wait: In the traversal, if a child c has S[c] > A[v], we set jump[c] = jump[v]. This means final from c is same as final from v. But what if jump[v] is not the final node from v in the "subtree absorbed" state because v's parent condition might fail? jump[v] already accounts for that. Good.

pop3 no children.
Answers: base1 false ->5; base2 true ->S[jump2]=S1=11; base3 true ->S3=1. Good.

Now, consider a node with two children. The traversal sets jump for both using same parent v. Is it possible that the condition for left child and right child differ? Yes, S[left] and S[right] different. We handle separately.

Now, let's think about the Cartesian tree with tie-break smaller index and the standard algorithm's parent assignment for `last`. In the loop:
if st:
    right[st[-1]] = i
    parent[i] = st[-1]
if last:
    left[i] = last
    parent[last] = i
Need ensure if `last` was previously right child of st[-1], setting right[st[-1]]=i overwrites it, and then left[i]=last preserves it. Good.

But what if `last` is 0 and st not empty, we set right[st[-1]]=i. If st[-1] already had a right child that was not popped? In a valid Cartesian tree, if st[-1] has a right child, that child would be on top of stack? Actually the stack contains the right spine. If st[-1] has a right child, that child is the next element in stack. If we didn't pop it, it means A[child] >= A[i]. But if st[-1] has a right child and we set right[st[-1]]=i, we would overwrite the existing right child. Is that possible? Let's see. The stack contains nodes on the right spine; if st[-1] has a right child, that right child is st[-2]? Wait stack order: bottom to top? In standard algorithm, stack is increasing? Let's recall. For max Cartesian tree with `while A[stack[-1]] < A[i]`, the stack maintains decreasing A from bottom to top? Example [5,1,3]: after i1 stack[1]; i2 stack[1,2] (A 5,1 decreasing); i3 pop2, stack[1], right[1]=3. So stack top is the rightmost node on the right spine. Thus the Cartesian tree parent is the smaller of the two nearest greater elements. The standard max Cartesian tree with tie-break indeed gives parent as the nearest greater element with smaller value (the "Cartesian tree" property). So the recurrence is sound.

Now, let's think about if the `parent` list is needed after building? We don't use parent after root? We don't use parent in DFS or jump? Actually we don't use parent after build except to find root? We use root=st[0]. We don't need parent for jump because we traverse children. We can avoid storing parent entirely? The standard algorithm needs parent to set for `last` and `i`? We can build left/right without parent? The standard algorithm uses parent for setting parent[last]=i and parent[i]=st[-1], but if we don't need parent later, we can omit parent list. However, the algorithm's correctness for left/right doesn't require storing parent? It uses parent to set relationships? Actually the standard algorithm sets left/right based on stack; parent is not needed to build left/right except maybe to update? Let's see: We can build left/right without parent:
```
last=0
while st and A[st[-1]] < ai:
    last=st.pop()
if st:
    right[st[-1]]=i
if last:
    left[i]=last
st.append(i)
```
Do we need parent? No, left/right are sufficient. The parent array is not used later. So we can omit parent to save memory. But in the standard algorithm, when we set right[st[-1]]=i, if st[-1] had a right child that was popped and is `last`, we overwrite; no parent needed. When we set left[i]=last, no parent needed. So parent is unnecessary. Let's confirm with [2,1,3] without parent:
i1 st[1]
i2 no pop, right1=2, st[1,2]

When popped, if not referenced elsewhere, it may be freed unless referenced in left/right. But left/right store references to some of these objects. Many indices may be referenced in left/right or stack. At most N int objects for indices, ~14MB. List refs: left+right ~8MB. Stack up to N refs ~4MB. L,R lists: L and R store int objects? L[i] = L[left[i]] references an int object from L list, not new. R similar. Initially zeros. Assignments reference existing int objects. Assign jump[c]=jump[v] references int object; jump[c]=c references int object c from loop variable? The loop variable c is a PyLong from left/right list, so references existing. So jump list refs ~4MB. base bytearray ~0.5MB. ans list of strings: 5e5 strings, each maybe ~50 bytes ->25MB plus list refs 4MB. Total maybe 120-150MB. Acceptable.

We can reduce memory by not storing S as list? We need S for jump and answers. Could compute S and store in list. OK.

We can reduce ans memory by using `io.StringIO` or generator? join needs list of strings. 5e5 strings memory okay. If memory tight, we can write in chunks. But likely fine. To be safe, we can build ans as list of strings; 5e5*~15 chars = 7.5MB char data + string overhead ~25MB. Still okay.

If we want to reduce memory, we can avoid `pref` list by computing S via subtree sums during DFS? But subtree interval sum can be computed from pref. We could compute S[v] by post-order sum of A in subtree without pref, using DFS and children. That would avoid pref list. Let's consider: We can compute S[v] = A[v] + S[left] + S[right] via post-order traversal. Since left/right children indices may be less/greater, we can do iterative post-order. This avoids pref list (18MB). We already have L,R? We need L,R for base_reachable. We can compute L,R and S in one post-order? L,R can be computed as described. S can be computed by post-order sum. Let's do that to save memory.

Compute S via post-order iterative:
We have left/right. We can compute S[v] = A[v] + S[left[v]] + S[right[v]] if children processed. Since left child index < v and right child index > v, we can compute S by processing nodes in reverse order? Let's see. For node v, left child index < v, right child index > v. If we process i from N down to 1, right child processed, but left child not. If we process i from 1 to N, left child processed, right child not. So simple order doesn't work. But we can do a post-order DFS using stack. Or we can compute S using L/R and pref (simpler). Memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can compute S with a post-order traversal without pref. Let's evaluate.

Post-order iterative:
stack = [(root, 0)] where state 0 = enter, 1 = exit.
On enter, push children. On exit, S[v] = A[v] + S[left[v]] + S[right[v]] (children already computed). This avoids pref. But we still need L/R for base. L/R can be computed with the increasing/decreasing passes without pref. So we can avoid pref entirely. Let's do that to reduce memory and maybe simplify? Post-order iterative is a bit more code but fine.

However, we need S for jump and answers. We can compute S in the same DFS as jump? Jump needs S[child] to decide. We can compute S bottom-up first, then jump top-down. So two traversals. We can combine? Maybe not easily because jump needs S of children, which requires bottom-up. We can do post-order to compute S, then pre-order to compute jump. That's two traversals. The L/R passes are O(N). Total O(N).

Let's implement post-order S:
```
S = [0]*(N+1)
stack = [(root, 0)]
while stack:
    v, state = stack.pop()
    if state == 0:
        stack.append((v, 1))
        if left[v]: stack.append((left[v], 0))
        if right[v]: stack.append((right[v], 0))
    else:
        s = A[v]
        if left[v]: s += S[left[v]]
        if right[v]: s += S[right[v]]
        S[v] = s
```
This uses stack of tuples, memory O(N) but tuples overhead. Could use two stacks or list of ints with sign. For N=5e5, tuple stack might be okay but we can optimize:
```
stack = [root]
order = []
while stack:
    v = stack.pop()
    order.append(v)
    if left[v]: stack.append(left[v])
    if right[v]: stack.append(right[v])
# order is preorder. Then process reversed(order) to compute S:
for v in reversed(order):
    S[v] = A[v] + (S[left[v]] if left[v] else 0) + (S[right[v]] if right[v] else 0)
```
This is simple. `order` list of N ints ~4MB refs + int objects? It references existing int objects. Good. Then we can delete order after S? We need order for nothing else. This avoids pref. Let's use this.

Then L/R computation still uses left/right. We can compute L/R before or after S. L/R passes O(N) no extra large memory.

Now, base computation uses L/R and A. Then jump traversal uses left/right and S. We can delete order after S.

Let's adjust code:

```
# Build left/right
...
root = st[0]

# L/R
L = [0]*(N+1); R = [0]*(N+1)
for i in range(1,N+1):
    L[i] = L[left[i]] if left[i] else i
for i in range(N,0,-1):
    R[i] = R[right[i]] if right[i] else i

# S via preorder order
order = []
stack = [root]
while stack:
    v = stack.pop()
    order.append(v)
    if left[v]: stack.append(left[v])
    if right[v]: stack.append(right[v])
S = [0]*(N+1)
for v in reversed(order):
    s = A[v]
    l = left[v]
    if l: s += S[l]
    r = right[v]
    if r: s += S[r]
    S[v] = s
del order
```
This computes S correctly because reversed preorder ensures children before parent? In a tree, preorder reversed is not always postorder? For a binary tree, reversed preorder (root, left, right) gives right, left, root, which is a valid postorder? Actually postorder is left, right, root. Reversed preorder (root, left, right) reversed = right, left, root. That's not the same as left, right, root, but it still processes children before parent? Yes, both children appear before parent in reversed preorder, so it's a valid bottom-up order. We don't care about left/right order, only children before parent. Since preorder pushes children after parent, in reversed order parent comes after children. Good.

Now, jump traversal:
```
jump = [0]*(N+1)
jump[root] = root
stack = [root]
while stack:
    v = stack.pop()
    av = A[v]
    c = left[v]
    if c:
        if S[c] > av:
            jump[c] = jump[v]
        else:
            jump[c] = c
        stack.append(c)
    c = right[v]
    if c:
        if S[c] > av:
            jump[c] = jump[v]
        else:
            jump[c] = c
        stack.append(c)
```

Now, we can delete left/right, L, R after jump? We need L/R for base already computed; base is bytearray. After jump, we don't need left/right, L, R. We can `del left, right, L, R` before building ans to reduce memory. But not necessary. We can do:
```
del left, right, L, R, stack
```
after jump. This helps memory before ans strings. But `stack` variable reused. Good.

We also don't need `st` after root; can `del st`.

Now, base computation:
```
base = bytearray(N+1)
for i in range(1,N+1):
    if L[i] == R[i]:
        base[i] = 1
    else:
        if i > L[i] and A[i-1] < A[i]:
            base[i] = 1
        elif i < R[i] and A[i+1] < A[i]:
            base[i] = 1
```

Now, answer:
```
ans = []
for k in range(1,N+1):
    if base[k]:
        ans.append(str(S[jump[k]]))
    else:
        ans.append(str(A[k]))
```
If we delete S? Need S for answers. Keep.

Now, let's consider if the final answer for K in a base true node but jump[K] is a node whose subtree interval is not the maximal final interval because K could absorb the parent's parent without absorbing the parent's other child if the other child is on the same side? In a binary tree, parent has at most two children: the child we came from and possibly another child on the other side. The grandparent is outside the parent's subtree. To reach grandparent, we must absorb the entire parent's subtree, including the other child, because the other child lies between parent and grandparent? Let's visualize: Parent p has left child c (our side) and right child d. The grandparent g is adjacent to the parent's subtree on one side. If p is left child of g, then g is to the right of p's subtree. p's right child d is between p and g? Actually p's subtree interval is [L_p, R_p], and g = R_p + 1 (if p is left child of g). The right boundary of p's subtree is R_p. If p has a right child d, then d's subtree is at the right end of p's subtree, so R_p is in d. Thus to reach g, we must absorb d's subtree. So yes. If p has no right child, R_p = p, and g is adjacent after absorbing p. So S[p] is sum before g. Good.

Now, let's think about if the final answer for K in a base true node but jump[K] is a node whose base is false and S[base
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
.

The final answer should be Python code only. I'll provide the code.</think>```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = [0] + data[1:1 + N]
    del data

    # Build max Cartesian tree.
    # Tie-break: smaller index is considered larger among equal values.
    left = [0] * (N + 1)
    right = [0] * (N + 1)
    st = []

    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        last = 0
        ai = A[i]
        while st and A[st[-1]] < ai:
            last = st.pop()
        if st:
            right[st[-1]] = i
        if last:
            left[i] = last
        st.append(i)

    root = st[0]
    del st

    # Subtree intervals
    L = [0] * (N + 1)
    R = [0] * (N + 1)

    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if left[i]:
            L[i] = L[left[i]]
        else:
            L[i] = i

    for i in range(N, 0, -1):
        if right[i]:
            R[i] = R[right[i]]
        else:
            R[i] = i

    # Subtree sums, computed bottom-up
    order = []
    stack = [root]
    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        order.append(v)
        if left[v]:
            stack.append(left[v])
        if right[v]:
            stack.append(right[v])

    S = [0] * (N + 1)
    for v in reversed(order):
        s = A[v]
        if left[v]:
            s += S[left[v]]
        if right[v]:
            s += S[right[v]]
        S[v] = s

    del order

    # base[i] = True iff starting from i, Takahashi can absorb his whole Cartesian subtree.
    base = bytearray(N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if L[i] == R[i]:
            base[i] = 1
        else:
            if i > L[i] and A[i - 1] < A[i]:
                base[i] = 1
            elif i < R[i] and A[i + 1] < A[i]:
                base[i] = 1

    # jump[v] = final node when we have already absorbed the whole subtree of v.
    jump = [0] * (N + 1)
    jump[root] = root
    stack = [root]

    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        av = A[v]

        c = left[v]
        if c:
            if S[c] > av:
                jump[c] = jump[v]
            else:
                jump[c] = c
            stack.append(c)

        c = right[v]
        if c:
            if S[c] > av:
                jump[c] = jump[v]
            else:
                jump[c] = c
            stack.append(c)

    ans = []
    for k in range(1, N + 1):
        if base[k]:
            ans.append(str(S[jump[k]]))
        else:
            ans.append(str(A[k]))

    sys.stdout.write(" ".join(ans) + "\n")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
